I’m up and moving around all day, going from one little job to the next, like I still have a crew waiting on me or something. It’s hard for me to just sit still and be done.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do that sometimes. Like in the storytelling meetings, I’ll jump in with an answer or start talking before the other person’s even finished. Then I catch myself and think, ah, I just cut them off, and I feel bad about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble paying attention to details or making careless mistakes?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. It’s not constant, but it happens. If I’m helping with the storytelling flyers, I might miss a date or leave out somebody’s name even though I thought I checked it. Same with my garden notes—I’ll write down a measurement wrong and not notice till later.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble sitting still or fidgeting when you're in quiet environments, like when you're watching TV or reading a book?\n\nPatient: Yes, especially in quiet places. If I’m sitting through a church talk or a meeting, I start shifting around, tapping my foot, or feeling like I need to stand up and stretch, even when I know I’m supposed to stay seated. It’s not awful, but it’s definitely there.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. It’s hard for me to just sit still and be done.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do that sometimes. Like in the storytelling meetings, I’ll jump in with an answer or start talking before the other person’s even finished. Then I catch myself and think, ah, I just cut them off, and I feel bad about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble paying attention to details or making careless mistakes?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. It’s not constant, but it happens. If I’m helping with the storytelling flyers, I might miss a date or leave out somebody’s name even though I thought I checked it. Same with my garden notes—I’ll write down a measurement wrong and not notice till later.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble sitting still or fidgeting when you're in quiet environments, like when you're watching TV or reading a book?\n\nPatient: Yes, especially in quiet places. If I’m sitting through a church talk or a meeting, I start shifting around, tapping my foot, or feeling like I need to stand up and stretch, even when I know I’m supposed to stay seated. It’s not awful, but it’s definitely there.\n\nDoctor: Have you always been this way, even as a child?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. Even back in grade school, teachers would say I was bright but kind of scattered. I was always losing pencils, calling out answers, and they had to remind me to finish my work before recess.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have mood swings or get easily upset?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t think I have big mood swings, at least not that I’ve noticed. I can get irritated sometimes, like anyone, but I wouldn’t say I’m easily upset most of the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you often talk more than others in social situations?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t think I talk a lot more than other people, at least not that I’ve noticed. Maybe sometimes if I’m comfortable, but I wouldn’t say it’s a big issue.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're always on the go, even when there's no need to be?\n\nPatient: Yeah, pretty much. Since I stopped working, I don’t seem to slow down much. I’m up and moving from one little job to the next all day, like I still have a crew waiting on me or something.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself dominating conversations or interrupting others when you're with friends or family?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do that sometimes. Like in the storytelling meetings, I’ll jump in with an answer before the other person’s even finished. Then I realize I cut them off, and I feel bad about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your difficulties with sitting still, impulsivity, or other behaviors are causing problems for you in your daily life or relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it does cause some problems. My wife has pretty much had to take over keeping the calendar and checking behind me, because I’ll miss things or not follow through. And I’ve had to apologize more than once to people at the community center or the garden group for letting them down. It’s not constant trouble, but it does get in the way.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that these behaviors, like not being able to sit still or acting on impulse, happen in all parts of your life, or are they mostly in certain situations?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I notice it more in some situations, like when I’m around people or trying to get things done, but I haven’t really kept track of whether it’s everywhere. It just kind of shows up here and there.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel restless or fidgety when you're doing quiet activities by yourself at home?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. If I’m trying to read or sketch or something quiet like that, I don’t usually stay settled for very long. I’ll start humming, talking to myself a little, or I’ll get up and fiddle with something around the house.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I don’t think I talk a lot more than other people, at least not that I’ve noticed. Maybe sometimes if I’m comfortable, but I wouldn’t say it’s a big issue.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're always on the go, even when there's no need to be?\n\nPatient: Yeah, pretty much. Since I stopped working, I don’t seem to slow down much. I’m up and moving from one little job to the next all day, like I still have a crew waiting on me or something.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself dominating conversations or interrupting others when you're with friends or family?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do that sometimes. Like in the storytelling meetings, I’ll jump in with an answer before the other person’s even finished. Then I realize I cut them off, and I feel bad about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your difficulties with sitting still, impulsivity, or other behaviors are causing problems for you in your daily life or relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it does cause some problems. My wife has pretty much had to take over keeping the calendar and checking behind me, because I’ll miss things or not follow through. And I’ve had to apologize more than once to people at the community center or the garden group for letting them down. It’s not constant trouble, but it does get in the way.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that these behaviors, like not being able to sit still or acting on impulse, happen in all parts of your life, or are they mostly in certain situations?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I notice it more in some situations, like when I’m around people or trying to get things done, but I haven’t really kept track of whether it’s everywhere. It just kind of shows up here and there.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel restless or fidgety when you're doing quiet activities by yourself at home?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. If I’m trying to read or sketch or something quiet like that, I don’t usually stay settled for very long. I’ll start humming, talking to myself a little, or I’ll get up and fiddle with something around the house.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these difficulties with impulsivity and restlessness consistently throughout your adult life?\n\nPatient: Not really throughout my whole adult life, no. It became more of a real problem after I retired from handyman work, about a year and a couple months ago. Since then I’ve noticed it more.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

It’s not awful, but it’s definitely there.\n\nDoctor: Have you always been this way, even as a child?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. Even back in grade school, teachers would say I was bright but kind of scattered. I was always losing pencils, calling out answers, and they had to remind me to finish my work before recess.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have mood swings or get easily upset?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t think I have big mood swings, at least not that I’ve noticed. I can get irritated sometimes, like anyone, but I wouldn’t say I’m easily upset most of the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you often talk more than others in social situations?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t think I talk a lot more than other people, at least not that I’ve noticed. Maybe sometimes if I’m comfortable, but I wouldn’t say it’s a big issue.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're always on the go, even when there's no need to be?\n\nPatient: Yeah, pretty much. Since I stopped working, I don’t seem to slow down much. I’m up and moving from one little job to the next all day, like I still have a crew waiting on me or something.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself dominating conversations or interrupting others when you're with friends or family?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do that sometimes. Like in the storytelling meetings, I’ll jump in with an answer before the other person’s even finished. Then I realize I cut them off, and I feel bad about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your difficulties with sitting still, impulsivity, or other behaviors are causing problems for you in your daily life or relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it does cause some problems. My wife has pretty much had to take over keeping the calendar and checking behind me, because I’ll miss things or not follow through. And I’ve had to apologize more than once to people at the community center or the garden group for letting them down. It’s not constant trouble, but it does get in the way.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that these behaviors, like not being able to sit still or acting on impulse, happen in all parts of your life, or are they mostly in certain situations?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I notice it more in some situations, like when I’m around people or trying to get things done, but I haven’t really kept track of whether it’s everywhere. It just kind of shows up here and there.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel restless or fidgety when you're doing quiet activities by yourself at home?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. If I’m trying to read or sketch or something quiet like that, I don’t usually stay settled for very long. I’ll start humming, talking to myself a little, or I’ll get up and fiddle with something around the house.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these difficulties with impulsivity and restlessness consistently throughout your adult life?\n\nPatient: Not really throughout my whole adult life, no. It became more of a real problem after I retired from handyman work, about a year and a couple months ago. Since then I’ve noticed it more.\n\nDoctor: Have you had problems with impulsivity, restlessness, or inattention since you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah, thinking back, I guess some of it was there when I was a kid. In grade school, teachers would say I was bright but scattered. I was always losing pencils, blurting out answers, and they had to remind me to finish my work before recess.\n\nDoctor: Have these difficulties significantly interfered with your relationships or daily activities?\n\nPatient: Yes, it has, not in a huge dramatic way, but enough that it causes problems. My wife has pretty much taken over keeping the calendar and checking behind me so I don’t miss things. And I’ve had to apologize more than once to folks at the community center and the garden group because I dropped the ball or let people down.\n\nDoctor: Did you have significant problems with impulsivity, restlessness, or inattention as a child that caused difficulties for you or those around you?\n\nPatient: Yes, I did have some of that as a kid. Like I said, even in grade school, teachers would say I was bright but scattered. I was always losing pencils, calling out answers, and needing reminders to finish my work before recess. It wasn’t constant trouble, but it did cause issues.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed these difficulties with impulsivity, restlessness, or inattention happening at work, at home, and in social situations?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to separate it out. I’m not working now, so I can’t really speak to a workplace these days. At home, my wife would probably say she notices it more than I do. Socially, I guess it comes up sometimes, but I don’t know if I’d say I’ve clearly noticed it in all those places.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
